- [x] **The log does not reproduce a row** (D692, found 2026-10-16 building §1's replay condition). ADR-0033 decision 2 requires that a fold of an object's events reproduce its row and lets a repair tool rebuild rows from the log; `storage-schema.md` §4 says the payload holds the writes and the inputs. The core's payload holds the inputs only: observed through the wire, two `bump`s assigning `count := count + inputs.by` and `stamp := now` leave `count: 10` and a stamp on the row and `{"inputs": {"by": 5}}` in each event. Re-execution does not repair it, since a sequence's gaps from refused creations are not in the log (ADR-0029). **For the author**, since it changes what every event holds and what every read of the log answers. (A) Each event records what it wrote: each attribute of its object it changed, with the value after (null where cleared), beside the inputs, as §4 already says; an erasure redacts a personal attribute's writes as it redacts its inputs; `history`, `events`, `pull` and `export` mask them as they mask personal inputs; the reference runner records the same, the tee holding the two alike; the harness folds the log. Its cost is a larger event (a creation's by its row) and four reads' masking, where a mistake would leak a personal value. (B) ADR-0033 amended to re-execution, with what re-execution cannot recompute (sequence values) recorded alone: smaller, but a row is then verified by the code that wrote it, so a defect in that code reproduces itself. Recommended: (A). The log as the history is the record's claim (PRD L2), and the harness's replay condition, the interval index's tracked members and ADR-0033's repair tool each need it. Meanwhile read sets and refusals are re-evaluated over rows the harness keeps itself, which needs neither. **Decided 2026-10-16** ("continue the loop with your recommendations"), ADR-0209 decisions 1 to 4: (A). *Built 2026-10-16: each event's payload holds `wrote`, measured by the world from each object's last event (`World::wrote`) at every site that records one, a transition, a filed, decided or invalidated proposal, a change, a redaction, an import, a migration and the store's creation; an erasure nulls a personal attribute's writes in the events before its own and the copy where the event that wrote it recorded it; an erased identity is replaced in what events wrote, a value, a set's elements and a proposal's inputs as text; `export` omits a personal attribute's writes and a proposal's personal inputs within its text. The core's suite found two leaks on its first run, both fixed: a proposal's `inputs` text exported with a personal input, and an erased identity an effect had copied. Held by the harness's replay condition, the core's tests folding the log after an import, a migration, an erasure, a clearing and a proposal's invalidation, and the deployment's test that no byte of an erased identity is in the store's files; seventeen mutants killed, the first-change snapshot's after a test of the world's contract was added.*

The shared key, which ADR-0178 decision 7's tests did not cover, is now held by `a_request_sent_at_once_under_one_key_applies_once` (a creation and a bump, twelve callers each), failing with the replay check skipped.* *And the command line probed the same day: every misconfiguration tried (a missing or malformed value, an unknown option, a directory or a foreign file as the store, a missing directory, a socket beside an address, bad durations and counts) meets a stamped sentence and exit 2, never a panic; one slip was not, an option given where a value belongs, `--store --create --create` making a store named `--create` and serving it, now refused naming both (`what_the_service_was_given_is_said`, a mutant).*

The runner now rounds where the core's evaluator does (`flowrun.py` `as_held`, typed by `held_arith_kind`, which mirrors `eval.rs` `combine`: a quotient at its dividend's scale, a literal of more places keeping them, an amount at its minor unit, a duration at whole seconds, two like quantities' quotient at six places, half to even), a quotient written to a decimal is divided once at its target's (`written`, as `quotient_at`), and the sandbox types a derived read as the evaluator holds it, so `d2 + 3` reads `"13.00"`. Held by `test_a_quotient_is_rounded_where_the_evaluator_rounds_it_alike` (three dividends, ten derived values, two writes), six mutants killed. Left: a quotient whose dividend is an input, an element of a set of values or a derived attribute read inside arithmetic is still exact on the runner, since its scale is not in the runner's types; an `if`'s second branch is typed as its first (R7); R2 to R6, R8, R9.* *R2 to R5 probed 2026-10-15 after the publish checks of K-1 to K-11: R2's amounts of two currencies and an amount compared with a number, and all of R3, are refused at publish, so neither backend runs them; R4, `max` over an enumeration's members, published and was `501` on the core and `LOW` by name on the runner, and both checkers now refuse it (check 24, as K-4), held by a case and a mutant of each; R5, a duration or a time past 64 bits and a decimal past 128 bits of units or 38 places, was answered by the runner and refused `value_in_range` by the core, and the runner now bounds them where it rounds (`as_held`), held by `test_a_value_past_what_its_type_holds_is_refused_alike` (seven values), four mutants killed. A decimal of more than 19 places whose value is whole and under 2^63 is not bounded there, a cost the check's precondition saves. Left: R6, R7's other cases, R8, R9.* *R6 to R9 closed 2026-10-15, and with them this line. R6: the core runs `inherited_parts` since K-11 was fixed, held by `test_a_part_a_base_declares_goes_with_its_subtype_alike`. R7: an `if` is typed by the branch it takes (`held_kind` given its names' values), where `if n > 1 then 1.5 else 2.25` failed the runner's read; and a derived category, `state.category` or `live`, read `["category", "live"]` where the core reads `"live"`, now written by its name (`values.py` `encode`). (A first draft of this line said the category case was no longer reachable, unprobed; a probe showed it was, and it is fixed instead.) R8: a time and each calendar bucket of a year below 1000 are written with four digits by the runner, the sandbox and `flowpublish.py`, where `%Y` wrote `999`. R9: a derived `this.intervals`, `this.intervals(member)` or `this.transitions` reads as the core's rows (`sandbox.py` `row_read`), where the runner failed the whole read `500`. Held by `test_a_year_before_1000_is_written_with_four_digits_alike`, `test_a_publish_reports_a_clock_before_1000_with_four_digits` and `test_a_derived_history_and_choice_are_read_alike`, ten mutants killed.*

G8 done 2026-10-15: probed one flow at a time, both checkers already refused a bucket in a derived attribute, a condition and a `set`. - [x] **A mirror's `imported_at` is admitted and never run** (found 2026-10-15 probing G9). `declaration-syntax.md` §8, `storage-schema.md` §4 and ADR-0100 §7 give every mirror object `.imported_at`, "the time its last import wrote it", for a freshness guard (`customer.imported_at + 1 h >= now`); the checker admits it (`check/names.rs` `MIRROR_MEMBERS`), but the store keeps no such column and both backends refuse it at run time (core: "the member `Employee.imported_at`", probed through the evaluator on an imported mirror). No flow reads it and the PRD requires no mirror freshness. The two decisions disagree on its meaning: ADR-0146 decision 7 made a refresh that changes nothing write nothing, so "the last import that wrote it" is the last that *changed* it, and the ADR's own guard would refuse a customer confirmed hourly whose record has not changed in a day. Options: (a) derive it from the object's latest `import` event, no schema change, and say it is when an import last changed it, rewriting the example; (b) a column every import naming the object stamps, changed or not, a write per unchanged refresh against ADR-0146; (c) **withdraw it before 1.0.0**: the checker refuses reading it, the name stays reserved against shadowing, and the documents and ADR-0100 say so, to add later with a use. Recommended (c): adding a member after 1.0.0 is additive and removing one is not, and the meaning a freshness guard needs is (b)'s, which nothing yet asks for. For the author, since it withdraws what ADR-0100 decided. **Decided 2026-10-16** ("go ahead with your recommendations"), ADR-0210 decision 3: (c). *Done the same day: both checkers refuse reading it, by its name on a mirror or through a reference, naming the withdrawal, and keep the name against shadowing; the documents that gave it say so. Withdrawing it found D695, any member a type does not declare read through a reference accepted, fixed with it.*

- [x] **Caller taint on the read side** (found 2026-10-15 probing the write path's last minor; the audit's "seen outside this area" line for area 1). An input not marked personal that a create or a call passes into another object's personal attribute is recorded in clear on the caller's event, and `history`, `events` and `export` answer it, where `export` omits every personal value and an input written into the object's own personal attribute is withheld (check 10). Probed on a flow where `Customer.add_contact.phone` creates `Contact.phone`, personal: the core's `add_contact` event and its export line hold `"phone": "555-0100"`. Erasing the contact redacts it there (S6 holds), but erasing the customer does not. Options: (a) **withhold it when the event is written**, `input_personal` reaching one hop through a create's or a call's inputs as `writes_of` does (D547), so it is recorded by name as withheld, in both backends; (b) omit it on the read side only, leaving it in the store's file. Recommended: (a), the same rule check 10 already applies to the object's own attribute, one hop on, which also lets the caller's own erasure owe nothing. It changes what an event records for such a flow, so it lands before 1.0.0. The runner answers no event payload, so the tee cannot compare it. Its test would check the core's event, its export and its attempt row. *Read and record side done 2026-10-15: `input_personal` reaches one hop through a create's or a call's inputs, a call's target read by every type taking its transition, so the export omits the input, a refused attempt withholds it, a request's digest masks it and an override's reason keeps it out, held by `an_input_passed_into_a_personal_attribute_is_personal_for_the_caller` and a mutant. **Left for the author:** what an erasure reaches through such an input. Applying the same rule to the erasure walk widened it, measured on the probe's flow: erasing the customer also cleared the contact's phone, another object's value that only passed through the customer's request. So the walk keeps its old rule, and the caller's own erasure still leaves the input on its own event, as before. Options: (i) the walk follows it, erasing what flowed on with it; (ii) the caller's own event is redacted but nothing it flowed into; (iii) as now. Recommended (ii): the caller's event holds the input as the person who sent it sent it, and the contact's phone is the contact's to erase. The reference runner answers no attempt row's values, so its half is untestable on the wire and is not changed.* *Decided 2026-10-16 ("go ahead with your recommendations"), ADR-0210 decision 4: (ii); done the same day, the erased object's own events losing an input passed into another object's personal attribute and the walk not following it, held by `an_input_passed_into_a_personal_attribute_is_personal_for_the_caller` and a mutant.*

**A question for the author** (2026-10-14), with a recommendation, which ADR-0205 decision 13 brings by name: metric reads that do not meet C5 at the profile.

16. **GATE-P's report readers.** ADR-0204 §1's profile has "four of them reading reports". The audit read it as four readers each sending a metric read the moment the last one answered, with no pause, and two agents doing the same. Gate P as `scripts/profile/load.py` plays it does so. Measured 2026-10-14 on the reference machine, the two-year synthetic store, at `bb241ed` plus the intervals change:
    - **Idle (Gate I), each read alone:** passes. The slowest metrics are 374 to 446 ms: the per-claimant ones and `time_in_state`.
    - **Back to back, 1x:** fails. Three metrics' p95 is 1.10 to 1.27 s. Writes p95 is 30 ms, one-object reads 7.7 ms, and there are no 5xx.
    - **Back to back, 2x:** fails. Every metric's p95 is 3 to 5.4 s, with four `503 read_timeout` (measured before the intervals change).
    - **The audit's paced variant** (`--think 1 --report-think 10`, a session pausing about 1 s between requests and a reader about 10 s between reports): **passes at 1x and at 2x**: at 1x measured before the intervals change, the slowest metric's p95 983 ms; at 2x after it, the slowest 882 ms, writes p95 47 ms and one-object reads 7.7 ms.

    Back to back, a read's latency is about the readers' count over the cores free for them, times its cost. Each speed-up is spent on more reads: the per-claimant metrics went from about 140 reads in 150 s to about 190 as they got faster, at the same p95. So the gate measures the machine's saturation more than a read's cost.

    Options:
    - **(a) The profile's readers have a pace.** Each report reader reads a report about every 10 s, an agent likewise, and a session pauses about 1 s, as the audit's own variant did. Gate P plays that, and back to back remains a stress run reported beside it, not a gate. ADR-0204 §1 says so.
    - **(b) Back to back stays the gate, and metrics become about three times cheaper.** That means metrics kept by the writer as it commits: an incremental aggregate per metric and group, a design of its own, which adds to each write's cost what it takes from each read, against A1.
    - **(c) A report may be answered from a computation at most N seconds old** (say 10), so readers back to back mostly share one. A product decision on staleness: a read would say its as-of time, as a guard's metric consult already does.

    **Decided 2026-10-16** ("go ahead with your recommendations"), ADR-0210 decisions 1 and 2: (a) for the gate, and (d) as the read's design. *Decision 1 done the same day: `load.py` plays the paced profile by default and `--stress` back to back, reported and never failing. Gate P paced at 1x on a two-year store built the same day by `build-store.py`, the release build at `68cfdbb` with D692's recorded writes and ADR-0208's grouped commits, on a shared 12-core machine: **passed**, writes p95 37.3 ms (132 ms on 2026-10-14 before ADR-0208), the slowest metric p95 775 ms, worklists p95 53.5 ms, one-object reads p95 8.1 ms, peak memory 802 MiB. The stress run beside it: writes p95 40.2 ms, four metrics' p95 1.14 to 1.44 s. Decision 2's limit stated in `operate.md`. Its window built the same day for a metric over objects whose time dimension is the item's own time or date: the objects read by that column from the window's start (`World::of_type_where_since`), the window still deciding each row, held by `a_window_over_an_objects_time_reads_by_its_column` and three mutants; measured on a sandbox store of 36,000 objects over six years, a 90-day window read in 5 ms where the whole history took 132 ms. Left: transitions and intervals narrowed by their windows, and the profile's reports read over a window by Gate P.* *Looked at the same day, and brought to the author: the log has no index on its events' times (`of_event` is indexed by object, cause, cursor, actor and principal), so narrowing a metric over transitions by its window needs one, `occurred_at` for the standard metrics, since a backdated event's position does not bound its time; every write would then keep it, on the writer ADR-0208 measured as the peak's bottleneck. Recommended: measure a write's cost with the index on the two-year store before choosing, and leave transitions whole-history meanwhile, a whole-history report beyond two years being the stated limit. Intervals have their entry time indexed, but the source reads the type's objects whole first; narrowing them is a change of its own, without a new index.* *Decided 2026-10-17, ADR-0211 decision 7: the write's cost measured first.*

    **Recommended: (a).** It is how the audit's own variant read the profile, and the only reading under which a faster read helps. It keeps every promise C5 makes for one read. (c) can be added later for reports at a scale (a) does not cover.

    **Added the same day, on the six-year store** (36,000 claims, 1,200 employees, built by `build-store.py --claims 36000`, at `da75c98`): the metrics fail **even one at a time**. The per-claimant ones are 1,454 and 1,585 ms, `time_in_state` 1,252 ms and `amount_claimed_by_category` 1,167 ms, about three times their two-year cost: a metric over a type's whole history is a scan, linear in the store. Paced at 1x they fail too (p95 1.36 to 1.96 s), while writes (p95 34 ms, 42 ms at 2x), one-object reads (p95 6.7 ms) and memory (2.4 GiB at 2x, against 8 GiB) hold. So (a) is necessary and not enough: C5's 1 s at six years for a whole-history report needs one of these:
    - **(d) Reports read over a window**, the rows narrowed by an index on the time dimension so a read's cost follows the window's activity, not the store's age. It cannot be measured on these stores, whose every event is recorded within minutes. The builder would need a clock it can set, which only a sandbox has.
    - **(b) Totals kept by the writer.**
    - **(c) A report answered from a recent computation.**

    Recommended: (a) for the gate's definition, and (d) as the read's design, with the profile's reports read over a window as a team's dashboard reads them. At the profile's rate, 36,000 claims in six years or about 6,000 a year, a 90-day window holds about 1,500 claims, an eighth of the two-year store, once the read narrows by the index rather than filtering every row. A whole-history report beyond two years is then a stated limit in `operate.md`, not a C5 promise, until (b) or (c) is chosen.

  - **Gates P and I on PostgreSQL, first runs, 2026-10-17** (release build at `f6bca3a`, the two-year profile store `build-store.py` builds, 12,000 claims and 600 employees from 95,924 requests, one 12-thread machine, NVMe, PostgreSQL 16.8 in Docker on the same machine, the load generator beside it). SQLite on a store built the same day by the same release: Gate I passed, Gate P passed (writes p95 38.7 ms, slowest metric p95 741.8 ms). PostgreSQL with its default settings: Gate I passed, its reads slower (one claim 3.9 ms against 1.0, the worklist 58.3 ms against 12.5); **Gate P failed**, `claimant_amount_claimed` p95 1,088 ms and `claimant_over_policy_lines` 1,128 ms past 1 s, writes p95 101.8 ms past 100 ms. With `shared_buffers` 1 GB on a fresh store it failed again (the two metrics 1,143 and 1,193 ms, writes 99.7 ms), so the server's cache was not the cause. Counted with `pg_stat_statements` over 60 s of Gate P's load: 215,781 statements, and the costliest, a quarter of the database's time (5,458 of 20,804 ms, 0.249 ms each), was the catalog read `not_null` made before every narrowed read of an object, 21,884 times (`store.rs`, `PRAGMA table_info`, a call in the process on SQLite); and a metric ranking its groups by the objects they name loaded each whole, 1,200 statements for the 600 claimants (`metric.rs`, `places`). Bypassing Docker's port proxy changed a metric by a tenth (720 to 649 ms), so the setup was not the cause either. **Fixed** the same day: the columns holding no null held by the connection on PostgreSQL by table and library version, let go when it changes a schema and as that transaction ends or rolls back to a savepoint (`Connection::not_null_held`, test `a_tables_shape_is_held_until_a_schema_changes`, five mutants killed); a metric's groups ranked by the directory's places read in chunks (`World::orders`, test `a_metrics_groups_by_an_object_are_in_creation_order`, which found that no test held groups by an object to creation order where ids' text orders them otherwise, its mutant surviving the whole suite before). Run again on a store the fixed release built (95,924 requests in 554 s), Docker's port proxy kept, as a database on another host costs more a round trip, not less: **Gate I passed** (the per-claimant metrics 584 and 580 ms, one claim 2.2 ms against 3.1 before, the worklist 29.8 ms against 54.6); **Gate P failed narrowly**, `claimant_amount_claimed` p95 1,073 ms and `claimant_over_policy_lines` 1,056 ms past 1 s, `time_in_state` 997 ms, writes p95 103.4 ms past 100 ms, one-object 9.5 ms, the worklist 68.9 ms against 101.7. SQLite with the same release, the same day: Gate I passed (the per-claimant metric 447 ms against 500), Gate P passed (writes p95 37.1 ms, slowest metric 716.3 ms).
    - **What the miss is, measured, and what it is not.** Over 60 s of Gate P after the fixes, 182,872 statements took 16.5 s of the database's time, against 215,781 and 20.8 s before. The machine was not saturated: the service used 33.4 CPU-seconds, PostgreSQL 29.8, Docker's proxy 15.5 and the load generator 3.2 over 74 s, on 12 threads. One statement's round trip is 0.051 ms through the proxy and 0.026 ms directly (`pgbench`, `SELECT 1`, one client), so a write's 35 to 40 statements (five `of_interval` inserts, four `of_attribute_write` upserts, three counters read and three written, three actor lookups, four checks of the store each request makes) cost about 2 ms of round trips here, more on a network; one writer serialises them, which is where p95 grows. No JIT compiled any statement. The `shared_buffers` 1 GB run changed nothing. The metrics at idle are within a fifth of SQLite's; under load they grow 1.8 times on PostgreSQL against 1.3 on SQLite.
    - **Found and left:** the worklist's query takes about 6 ms in PostgreSQL alone (25 ms a call under load), its planner walking the object directory in creation order, 4,460 rows, to find the first 51 submitted claims, where the state index holds 576; no gate bounds a worklist.

    - [x] **The write path's statements batched**, 2026-10-17: the allocator's three counters and the restore's floor read in one statement and the counters written in one, the spans a write touched upserted in statements of many rows, the touched ones before the merged ones are deleted as before and the change's own where they were, and the attribute-write index likewise, each keeping the last of a key named twice, as one statement each did; about twelve round trips fewer a write. Test `a_span_or_a_write_named_twice_in_one_write_keeps_the_later`, whose two mutants (the first kept) survived the whole suite before it. `0cd21f8`. **Measured again 2026-10-18**, the 0.3.0 release build, fresh two-year stores on both backends, the machine idle at the start (load 0.3 and 0.9): on PostgreSQL Gate I passed and Gate P's writes passed, p95 94.5 ms against 103.4 before, one-object 15.8 ms, the worklist 75.7 ms, the metrics within 1 s but `claimant_over_policy_lines`, p95 1,029 ms over 11 answers, **the one miss, accepted by name for 0.3.0** (decision (a) above; `operate.md` says it); on SQLite both passed, writes p95 35.4 ms, the slowest metric 725.2 ms.
- [x] 7. **The reviews' findings on PostgreSQL**, each probed by its reviewer on a database of its own (the reports name each probe, its method and its output; "inferred" where they say so), fixed in groups, each gated and committed. Two reviewers found the first independently. *Closed 2026-10-19 with h.*
  - [x] **a. The database's locale.** The store refused a database by its collation alone (`db.rs`, `datcollate`), so one with another `LC_CTYPE` or the ICU provider was taken, where `lower()` folds `Ä` to `ä`: `identity_taken` was bypassed, a known actor refused, and an erasure of one person renamed another's identity to the erased object's id (records F2, personal data 7). *Fixed 2026-10-17, `2df9076`: the collation, the character type and the provider each checked, the guide's `CREATE DATABASE` naming `LOCALE_PROVIDER libc`; test `a_database_sorting_or_folding_text_beyond_bytes_is_refused`, a mutant of each check killed.*
  - [x] **b. Transactions and the connection.** A lock wait on PostgreSQL happens at a statement, never at `BEGIN`, and its busy mark was dropped turning it into the store's error (`store.rs`, `From<db::Error>`), so a write, an acknowledgement or a publish meeting a lock answered `500 internal` after 5 s, never `stale`; a publish fails beside any read holding its table 5 s, `pg_dump` included; an erasure whose VACUUM met a lock committed and was answered 500, and an unkeyed retry recorded a second erasure (records F3, durability 7). A lost connection is never reopened: after a database restart, a terminated backend or an idle timeout every write answers 500 until a person restarts the service, while the watchdog is fed (durability 1). No connect, statement or keepalive timeout is set: a 40 s stall of the database hung every request and `/health`, reads past `--read-timeout` (durability 3). A vanished host's session keeps the store's advisory lock about 2 h 11 min, the server's keepalive defaults (durability 4). `synchronous_commit` is not pinned (durability 8). Every connection rewrites the catalog functions before taking the lock, so a refused start changes the database and concurrent connects can fail "tuple concurrently updated" (durability 9). A `COMMIT` of a transaction a failed statement aborted is answered `ROLLBACK` without an error, and taken as committed (records F7, inferred, no wire trigger found). *Fixed 2026-10-17, `25259e7`: a lock wait kept as busy, a request answered `stale`, `retries_exhausted`, and a publish or any other operation 503, test `a_write_meeting_another_sessions_lock_is_answered_stale` and `a_publish_beside_a_read_on_postgresql_is_answered_unavailable`; a lost connection answered 503 and the store opened again, its lock taken again, before the next write, a read connection lost dropped from the pool, test `a_store_on_postgresql_whose_connection_is_lost_is_opened_again`; a connect waited 5 s, the client's keepalives and a 30 s bound on unacknowledged data, the server's keepalives and `synchronous_commit = on` given in each session's start, the catalog made once under a mark and a lock, a database that takes no writes refused to the writer, test `a_connection_to_postgresql_holds_its_settings_and_leaves_the_catalog`; a commit of a transaction a failed statement aborted refused and rolled back, test `a_transaction_a_failed_statement_aborted_is_not_committed`; and, since a store now opened again by itself would write on whatever database its connection string names after a failover, a database not the one the store was in at the start (another system identifier, timeline or oid) latches the store unserved until a person marks it a copy, test `a_store_on_postgresql_replaced_in_place_is_served_no_more`; eleven mutants, each killed.* **Left:** a database that stops answering without closing its connections still holds every request waiting on it, `/health` among them, since the synchronous client bounds no statement's wait (the guide says so); `/health` answered from a view kept beside the writer is the remedy. *`/health`'s part fixed 2026-10-18: its read of the store runs on a thread of its own, one at a time, and is waited for 5 s, so a stalled store answers `503 store_stalled`, at once while the read still runs, and `200` once the store answers again; test `a_stalled_database_is_said_by_health`, through a proxy that stops passing bytes with its connections open, as `docker pause` does (no answer in 20 s before the fix), three mutants killed. The other requests still wait with the database, which the guide says.*
  - [x] **c. Names past 63 bytes.** PostgreSQL cuts every identifier to 63 bytes and the store's generated names are unbounded (`t_<type>__<attr>`, `<table>_<col>_ix`, set-asides' `__v<n>`), so two set attributes of a 38-character type shared one side table, made `IF NOT EXISTS`: a document lost, and the log recording a write never made; two references' index names collided and a publish SQLite takes answered 500 (records F1). *Fixed 2026-10-17, `25259e7`: every piece of DDL sent to PostgreSQL is scanned, and a name past 63 bytes outside its literals and comments is refused before it is sent, a version or a sandbox needing one answered `409`, where SQLite takes it; tests `a_name_past_postgresqls_63_bytes_is_found` (63 taken, 64 refused, quoted, literals and comments passed over) and `a_store_whose_names_postgresql_would_cut_is_refused_there` (the reviewer's 38-character type); two mutants killed.* **Left:** names that fit by a stable short form, so such a flow publishes on PostgreSQL too, which changes `storage-schema.md`'s names and is the author's to weigh.
  - [x] **d. Inputs PostgreSQL cannot hold.** U+0000 in `context`, an idempotency key, an actor or a transition is stored by SQLite and a 500 on PostgreSQL, the refusal's attempt row lost (records F5); text past 2,704 bytes in an indexed column, a unique or indexed string, an identity, an unknown actor or transition in the attempt's index, is a 500 (records F4). *Fixed 2026-10-17, `25259e7`: U+0000 anywhere in a request's text but an input's or an attribute's value, which the engine refuses `invalid_input` as before, and a name past 1 KiB (an actor's id or principal, a type, a transition, an object's id, a legacy or an idempotency key), refused `400` at the wire on both backends, so they answer alike and the attempt log's index holds every refusal's row; a value of a unique, indexed or identity attribute past the 2,704 bytes an index's entry holds answered `409` on PostgreSQL, naming the bound, where it was a 500; `wire.md`, `errors.md` and `operate.md` say so; tests `what_no_store_can_hold_is_malformed_at_the_wire` and `a_value_past_postgresqls_index_entry_is_a_conflict`; three mutants killed.* **Left:** such a value is still taken by SQLite and refused by PostgreSQL; a bound the engine holds on both, so they agree, is the author's to weigh.
  - [x] **e. Erasure on PostgreSQL.** Plain VACUUM does not zero the space it frees, and the erasure rewrote an event row once per personal attribute, each version holding the others: erased values were found in the free space of heap and index pages after the service's VACUUM and CHECKPOINT in every flow probed, 2 of 7 copies still there after 2,000 more writes (personal data 1). Past about 50 pages VACUUM bypasses index cleanup, leaving an erased email a live index entry (2). Autoanalyze's samples keep erased names and emails in `pg_stats` (3). `maintain`'s `rebuild_indexes` is `REINDEX "name"`, a syntax error on PostgreSQL, so the daily housekeeping fails there, and it skips the indexes backing keys (4). The vacuum is never checked: a standby with feedback, a replication slot or a reader pinning a page leaves whole dead versions, unsaid (5), and a vacuum due when the service is killed is forgotten (durability 5). `operate.md`'s limits misstate the bounds: recycled WAL keeps old content, slots keep WAL unbounded, the erasure's own WAL carries the values, a physical backup taken after holds them, a logical dump does not (6); statement logging writes values (8); an idle store's due vacuum waits for a write (9). An erasure's rewritten payload comes back in jsonb's key order (records F6).
    - **Retracted, 2026-10-17:** ADR-0213 decision 8's "no dead version of an erased value stays in the live database" and `operate.md`'s "no erased value stays in the data files" rested on my probe of three values, each written once and nulled by one UPDATE, none found after VACUUM; I generalised it past what it measured, and the reviewer's probes contradict it.
    - [x] **Author: decision 8 on PostgreSQL**, since VACUUM alone cannot keep the live database free of erased bytes. *Taken as recommended, (i), on 2026-10-17 under the author's standing direction, and standing for the author to change (ADR-0213 decision 8, refined).* Options: (i) the daily housekeeping rewrites the tables erasures wrote (`VACUUM FULL`, every index with them), so none is left past a day, SQLite's bound for its indexes (`operate.md`, "None is left past a day"), an exclusive lock on each table for its rewrite in the maintenance window; (ii) the live database's free space stated as a limit beside the backups', bounded by later writes alone; (iii) a key per subject destroyed at erasure, rejected by ADR-0213. **Recommended: (i)**, the bound the store already promises on SQLite.
    - *Fixed 2026-10-17, `25259e7`:* the erasure's vacuum runs with `INDEX_CLEANUP ON` and `ANALYZE`, counts a walsender's, a slot's and a prepared transaction's snapshot among its holders and skips while one holds, checks the log's dead rows after, is marked due in the erasure's own transaction and run at the next open, retried every 5 s on PostgreSQL and said once it has waited a minute, and never turns a committed erasure into a fault; `rebuild_indexes` on PostgreSQL writes every table anew with `VACUUM FULL` and `pg_statistic` last; `operate.md`'s limits rewritten (the log, slots, archives and physical backups, statement logs, the data directory). Tests: `an_erasure_on_postgresql_vacuums_once_no_older_snapshot_holds_it` (now also `pg_stats`), `an_erasures_vacuum_due_at_a_stop_runs_when_the_store_is_opened_next`, `an_erasure_on_postgresql_leaves_no_byte_once_the_housekeeping_has_run` (reading the store's files: three held the erased value after the vacuum, none after the housekeeping); six mutants killed. **Left, and fixed 2026-10-18:** the erasure rewrote a log row once per personal attribute and part and through `jsonb` on PostgreSQL, so its payload's keys came back reordered, shortest first, on the wire too, since the engine keeps a payload's key order (records F6), and more copies were made before the housekeeping. Every JSON rewrite of an erasure, an event's payload, a proposal's inputs and an attempt's values, is now an edit in Rust over the rows a query finds, written back in batched statements, each row once a pass, where it was once for each attribute and part; tests `an_erasure_keeps_a_payload_as_it_was_written` (failing on the old rewrite on PostgreSQL with the reordered text) and `an_erased_identity_leaves_a_corrections_record` (S17, which no core test held); eight mutants killed, the position rule moved to the edit alone so the old test guards it again. Holders through a standby, a slot or a prepared transaction are counted but not tested, the test database having none. How long the housekeeping's rewrite takes at the profile's size is not measured.
  - [x] **f. A copy taken for the live store.** *Fixed 2026-10-17: a database replaced under a running service is latched unserved (b, `25259e7`); and a store keeps the database it was last opened in, its cluster's system identifier, timeline and oid, in its counters, so a start in another, unmarked, is refused (`not_its_database`), a marked one starts as a restore and says so as a file's does, and its database is then its own; `operate.md` gives the restore's commands, run against a store made by the release binary, and a backup's check; test `a_store_on_postgresql_restored_in_place_is_refused_until_marked`, four mutants killed.* No PostgreSQL backup can carry ADR-0207's mark, so marking is an `INSERT` at restore, the act ADR-0207 rejected as the one most likely forgotten; unmarked, a copy handed out 41 of 41 ids, positions and claim numbers again and skipped 80 of 100 events past a kept cursor; the start says nothing of a restore it did; and a failover to an asynchronous standby is such a copy, its last acknowledged writes lost, which nothing asks to prevent (durability 2).
  - [x] **g. Upgrades on PostgreSQL.** `release::upgrade` takes a SQLite file path, the start refuses a store at an earlier release instead of serving `upgrade_due`, and `/v1/upgrade` always says nothing is due: no effect in 1.0.0, whose PostgreSQL stores are all made at it, but every one is stranded by the first release after it; `operate.md` says the upgrade runs as on a file (durability 6). *Fixed 2026-10-18, for 0.4.0, which ADR-0214 decision 3 gates on it: the upgrade takes a PostgreSQL store, its steps the same over the facade, the store's advisory lock held for it and the upgrade refused where a service holds it; a service started on a store at an earlier release serves the upgrade alone and reopens the store after, its lost-connection check leaving a store due an upgrade alone; tests `a_store_on_postgresql_is_upgraded_through_each_release_between` (a registry with a release after 0.3.0) and `a_store_on_postgresql_at_an_earlier_release_is_upgraded` (a store taken back to 0.2.0's shape, upgraded through the service); two mutants killed.*
  - [x] **h. The rest.** *Fixed 2026-10-17: the stop's checkpoint and its words left to a file's store; the guide's quick check said to do nothing on PostgreSQL, with data checksums in its place; the restore's commands and a backup's check given (with f); the release's fingerprint digesting `PG_SCHEMA`, `PG_CATALOG` and the translated schema; a sandbox's schema named for its machine, so another machine's is never dropped as orphaned, test `a_sandbox_another_machine_made_is_left`; an empty list translated as SQLite reads it, `IN (NULL) IS TRUE`, and negated `IS NOT TRUE`, test `an_empty_list_matches_as_sqlite_reads_it`. Left: `/health`'s full-volume cause after a database's return, not probed again since b (*probed 2026-10-18: after a lost connection the write is answered `503 unavailable` naming PostgreSQL's reason, neither SQLite's log nor a full volume, and `/health` `200`, now asserted by `a_store_on_postgresql_whose_connection_is_lost_is_opened_again`*); the deployment suite run on one backend but for its PostgreSQL tests (*since 2026-10-18 a kill at three moments of an import, a stop with a request in flight, a stalled database, an upgrade, a restore, a replaced database and a lost connection each run on PostgreSQL: `an_import_killed_midway_on_postgresql_leaves_all_or_none`, `a_stopped_service_on_postgresql_answers_the_requests_in_flight`, the kill and the stop sharing their bodies with the tests on a file; the rest runs on a file alone*); `REFERENCES of_object`, harmless while the engine checks the type.* Messages and `/health` on PostgreSQL naming SQLite's log or a full volume after a lost connection, the guide's quick check a no-op there, no restore steps or check of a copy (durability 10); the release fingerprint not hashing `PG_SCHEMA`, `PG_CATALOG` or the translation (11, inferred); one of the service's 83 deployment tests run on PostgreSQL, none killing it, restarting the database, restoring or upgrading (12); `drop_orphaned_sandboxes` dropping another host's live sandbox on a shared server (personal data, outside); `read_log_after`, `read_attempts_after` and `read_interval_page` building `IN ()` from a type list not guarded against empty (records, outside, not probed); a reference to a type table compiled as `REFERENCES of_object` on PostgreSQL, a weaker check than SQLite's against decision 7's letter (records, harmless while the engine checks the type). *Closed 2026-10-19, the last part kept as built: a reference to a type's table is `REFERENCES of_object` on PostgreSQL, where a flow's references may form a cycle and PostgreSQL refuses a key to a table not yet made (`db.rs`, the translation's comment); the key still holds that the object exists, and the engine, the store's only writer, checks a reference's type, as it does where the target has subtypes (`storage-schema.md` §3, which now says so for PostgreSQL). A key to each type's table would be added after every table is made, a change to the store's schema and so a release's, checking nothing the engine does not.*
  - [x] **i. Author: a read's deadline on a frozen database.** A read is promised an answer at its deadline, `503 timed_out` (ADR-0184 decision 3), but on a PostgreSQL that stops answering with its connections open, frozen or stalled on its disk, the deadline's cancel request is never acted on and the read waits with the database, past it; `/health` now says the stall (`store_stalled`, 2026-10-18), the reads do not. The synchronous `postgres` client bounds no statement (its source has `connect_timeout` and `tcp_user_timeout` alone, and a frozen server's kernel still takes what is sent, so neither fires). Options: (a) leave it a stated limit, `/health` the signal; (b) the service runs each read on a thread of its own and answers at the deadline, the thread left blocked until the database answers, bounded by the reads' slots and a cap of abandoned reads, refused `store_stalled` past it: every read pays a thread's hop, and a stall holds threads; (c) the facade's PostgreSQL backend calls `tokio-postgres` itself, which the `postgres` crate wraps and the tree already holds, so each statement of a read connection is bounded on the client and the connection dropped at the deadline, nothing left blocked, writes left to wait as now, since a commit cut short has an outcome no one knows. **Recommended: (c)**, a change to the facade's PostgreSQL layer, every PostgreSQL test its check; it touches the hot path of every read on PostgreSQL, so it is the author's to take. *Decided 2026-10-18 by the author ("go with (c)"), ADR-0215, and built the same day: the facade's PostgreSQL client is `tokio-postgres` on a runtime of each connection's own, a read connection's every statement and its making bounded by the read's deadline, the connection closed there, a writer's making by 30 s and its statements by nothing; tests `a_connection_never_answered_is_given_up_at_its_deadline`, `a_read_connection_stops_at_its_deadline`, and `a_stalled_database_is_said_by_health`'s reads through the freezing proxy, each answered `503 read_timeout` by its 8 s deadline.*

**F. The release gate**: 0.3.0's (ADR-0214 decision 2), 0.4.0's (the same gate, with decision 3's upgrade from 0.3.0 on both backends), 0.5.0's, 0.5.1's and 0.5.2's (the same, each its upgrade from the release before), then 1.0.0's (ADR-0204 §7, §8).
- [x] The version raised to 1.0.0 with its release entry and a 0.2.0-to-1.0.0 upgrade fixture. *Done 2026-10-14 with the durability area: the entry adds `of_restore` and the interval retractions; a later schema change before the tag changes the entry and its fingerprint, since no 1.0.0 store exists yet.* **Renumbered 0.3.0 on 2026-10-17** (ADR-0214, at the author's decision): the entry and its step from 0.2.0 as they were, its fingerprint now digesting what a store on PostgreSQL holds too (durability on PostgreSQL 11).
- [x] **Author: PostgreSQL in the release** (asked 2026-10-17: "I need postgresql to be in the release"; **decided the same day**, "we need postgresql in 1.0.0 for the first deployment", option (a), and erasure's standard as recommended, ADR-0213; the rest as recommended at the author's direction to iterate until it is complete). *The blocks are tracked under "PostgreSQL in 1.0.0" below.* The design names PostgreSQL as the store, SQLite for embedded use (ADR-0001, `DESIGN.md` §2, `storage-schema.md`); the build made one SQLite file (ADR-0174 decision 2, which rejected PostgreSQL first), with no layer between the engine and the database: 252 SQL calls in `store.rs` and `release.rs`, 71 SQLite constructs in `store.rs` alone. Beyond the dialect, erasure's removal of bytes (SQLite rewrites and truncates its log; PostgreSQL keeps dead rows until VACUUM, and its log, replicas and backups copies), concurrency (one writer against serialisable transactions with retries, `DESIGN.md` §6), the sequence store, backup and restore, and the upgrade are SQLite's own, and every piece of the release's evidence is SQLite's. Options: (a) PostgreSQL in 1.0.0, a storage trait carved with SQLite behind it and the suite unchanged, PostgreSQL implemented behind it with every test on both, its erasure designed, the audit's storage areas run on it, then the gate; (b) 1.0.0 on SQLite, PostgreSQL in 1.1, additive on the wire and the format, with a move of a store from one to the other; (c) the seam carved now and 1.0.0 on SQLite, which reworks the audited core before the release and still ships none. Recommended: (a) where the first production deployment must run on PostgreSQL, (b) where it is an operations standard or later scale. Asked of the author: what PostgreSQL buys (a managed database, availability, several instances writing, scale, policy); whether "the release" is the 1.0.0 tag or the operations platform's first production deployment; whether SQLite stays beside it; and whether erasure on PostgreSQL must reach its log and backups as SQLite's does, or VACUUM with a stated limit suffices.

## Issues filed on GitHub

Filed on GitHub on 2026-10-18 by the operations platform's developer, each reproduced at 0.3.0; the author asked them addressed properly, "so that they don't have to work around it", and answered in the issue where one is by design with a good answer already.

- [x] **#22, check 33 misses seven members an object answers**, so an attribute named `attempts`, `type` or `labels` was shadowed in every expression, a guard on a counter `attempts == 0` silently false. *Fixed 2026-10-18 by ADR-0216, otherwise than the issue proposed: refusing the seven names would refuse `issues.yaml`'s `WorkItem.labels`, a shipped sample in format 1's frozen corpus. A member read by a path alone, added after format 1, yields to a type's own member of its name, as the checkers already typed it, and step 3 notes it, `hides_member`; one list in each engine, which check 33 reads and the evaluator answers by. Tests `an_attribute_named_like_a_later_member_is_the_types`, the issue's counter, and `every_listed_member_is_answered_and_no_other`; two mutants killed; the checkers' oracle agrees on the notice.*
- [x] **#26, `/sandbox/load` installs a flow the checker refuses**, the first request reaching the refused construct answered `500`. *By design and documented (`quickstart.md`: "the service does not check what a sandbox loads, so always run the checker first"), and fixed 2026-10-18 all the same, since the documented answer was the workaround: the core's service checks a load with its own checker, which agrees with the reference checker by the oracle, its modules without their examples, and refuses with the findings in the shape a file that does not parse is refused in; `"unchecked": true` installs as before, on the studio's server too; the three guides that said a load is unchecked corrected. Test `a_sandbox_load_refuses_what_the_checker_refuses`, the issue's flow.*
- [x] **#25, `only_via` cannot say "only as a consequence"** without naming the importing module. *Fixed 2026-10-18 by ADR-0217: `only_via: [any]`, taken by every transition of any type and refused to a direct request, in the schema, both checkers (alone in its list, a part's creation still naming its whole), the core and the reference runner; `any` rather than the issue's `cascade`, a word the format already gives a whole's final transitions reaching its parts. Test `a_transition_only_via_any_is_taken_by_any_other`, the issue's two modules, and a plant of `any` beside a named transition; the issue's modules with three examples pass on the reference runner.*
- [x] **#24, a query's filter cannot state an absolute date or time**: bound parameters. *Fixed 2026-10-18 for `query` by ADR-0218: a filter names `:name` and `params` gives its value, bound after the parse to the value decoded as the member it meets is declared, as a request's input is, so the filter's text holds no value and its answer no clock's offset; the personal guard and the cost guard parse it alike, so a parameter on a personal member is personal. Test `a_filters_parameters_are_read_as_their_members`, the issue's desk bookings. **Left:** a metric's filter, whose rows' fields need a table by source to type a parameter.*
- [x] **A metric's filter takes parameters** (#24's rest), typed by its source's rows: an object's members for `objects` and observations, and the spans', events' and attempts' fields.
  - [x] The query's binder takes what the compared side reads as, so a metric's filter binds by its rows as a query's binds by its type. *Done 2026-10-19: `bound_by`, `member_reads`; the query's own test passes, and a mutant blinding the query's reading is killed by it.*
  - [x] Each source's row fields typed as `declaration-syntax.md` §6.9 lists them, a path through the row's object or subject reaching that type's members. *Done 2026-10-19 (`row_reads`, ADR-0221): `.reason` and `.held(...)` read as nothing, the second since a reader's filter cannot name it (D569). A label's subject state was first typed as a state, which a parameter would never equal, its row holding the text; the test's equivalence over it caught it.*
  - [x] `/v1/metric` takes `params`, refused as a query's are, its cursor carrying them; `wire.md`, `wire-v1.json`, an ADR and the changelog. *Done 2026-10-19 by ADR-0221; a metric's page has no cursor, so nothing carries them. Tests `a_metric_filters_operands_read_as_its_rows_fields`, `a_metric_filters_parameters_are_read_as_its_rows_fields` and the service's `a_reads_parameters_are_taken_at_the_wire`, the first service test of a query's `params` too; ten mutants of the final code killed, and one survivor removed as redundant (ADR-0221).*
- [x] **The evaluator's recursion has little stack to spare in a debug build.** `a_recursive_derived_value_and_a_wide_identifier_stop_without_a_crash`, a derived value recursing to `DERIVED_HOPS` (64), overflowed the test thread's 2 MiB on PostgreSQL when #24 added two arms to `eval.rs`'s `term`, whose frame every level of an expression holds; moving the new arm's refusal out of the frame (`#[cold]`) restored it. *Measured 2026-10-18 by `RUST_MIN_STACK` on that test: a debug build needs between 1,792 and 2,048 KiB on either backend, a release build, which the service runs, between 256 and 384 KiB, against threads of 2 MiB.* To weigh: the service's runtime threads given a larger stack, or the recursion bounded by its depth in frames, so a debug build's tests do not sit on the edge. *Corrected 2026-10-19: this said the release build's need stood "against threads of 2 MiB"; the service runs a request on a thread of 16 MiB (`WORKER_STACK`, `objectflow-service`'s `main.rs`), and the 2 MiB is the test harness's, so the edge was the test's alone, and neither option weighed above was needed.* *Fixed 2026-10-19: the test runs on a 16 MiB thread, as `a_chain_of_calls_stops_without_a_crash` does; it passes with the harness's default stack at 1 MiB, where it overflowed below 1,792 KiB, and a mutant raising `DERIVED_HOPS` to a million still aborts it.*
- [x] **#23, a personal attribute's name makes same-named members of every other type personal in queries.** *Fixed 2026-10-18 by ADR-0219, as the issue's first design proposed: a query's filter and order judge each member as the type it belongs to declares it, the queried type, a reference path's or an aggregate's binding's, a derived attribute personal where it reads a personal member it reaches, and only a member whose type the filter does not tell by its name in any type. The guides said nothing of the name-wide rule, which the issue found in the code; `wire.md` now states the rule. Test `a_querys_members_are_personal_as_their_own_types_declare`, the issue's office; two mutants killed; found too in #24's test, where the built-in `Label`'s personal `note` made every type's `note` personal. **Left:** the guards of `available`, `check` and `availability`, whose transitions' guards are still judged by name, in both backends.*
- [x] **The guards a transition's answer reads judged personal by type**, as a query's are since ADR-0219, in the core. *Retracted 2026-10-19: this said "in the core and the reference runner alike", after ADR-0219 decision 4's "a change to both backends"; neither was checked. The reference runner has no guard on `check`, `availability` or `available` (`flowrun.py`'s one personal guard is a metric filter's, line 1566), as ADR-0206's consequences say, so the guard is the core's alone.*
  - [x] A guard's and an invariant's members judged as `Personal` judges a query's, and an input read as `inputs.<name>` by the transition's own declaration, where a name personal in any type or transition made it personal. *Done 2026-10-19 by ADR-0220: test `a_transitions_guards_are_personal_as_their_own_types_declare`, four mutants killed (inputs by name, the identity input dropped, invariants not judged, the old judgement whole); the service's `a_read_by_a_personal_value_is_opted_into` still refuses Person.promote's guard on a personal name.*
  - [x] ADR-0219 decision 4 corrected where it said both backends; `wire.md`, an ADR and the changelog. *Done 2026-10-19: ADR-0219's decision 4 retracted in place, ADR-0206's and ADR-0219's refined-by lines, ADR-0220.*
- [x] **#27, an upgrade after its dry run on one service is refused `503 store_replaced`** on a file store, as `operate.md`'s steps 4 and 5 have it, and the service then served nothing until a restart, its fault telling the operator to restore a store nothing had harmed; filed 2026-10-19 by a consumer upgrading 0.3.0 to 0.4.0. *Found: a store due an upgrade is not open, deliberately, and the service notes the write-ahead log beside it at its start, which a stopped 0.3.0 service leaves, empty; the dry run's own connection, the only one open, removed it as it closed, and the upgrade's take of the writer found the noted log gone. Fixed 2026-10-20: the upgrade notes the log again after its own connection closes, whether it ran, rolled back or was refused, as the service's own act. Test `an_upgrade_after_its_dry_run_runs_on_the_same_service`, which failed with the issue's fault before the fix: a 0.3.0 fixture store with an empty log beside it, held by no one. The upgrade's own test runs a dry run first too and never met it, each fixture being kept as one file with no log (`docs/LESSONS.md`).*
- [x] **#28, an `unsatisfied` verdict names no input**, so a form could not mark the field a guard refused, where an `invalid_input` names each; filed 2026-10-20: a rule over a booking's two days, refused, marked neither day's field. *Done 2026-10-20 (ADR-0224): the verdict answers `inputs`, each declared input its clause reads in the transition's order, a generated `<attribute>_provided` its input, any other clause none; the Flow Studio's runner backend answers the same, its tests comparing it with the core having failed until it did. Tests `an_unsatisfied_clause_names_the_inputs_it_reads` (core) and `an_unsatisfied_verdict_names_the_inputs_its_clause_reads` (service, `request` and `check`), each failing a mutant of the order, the guard restriction, `_provided` and the service's field. Not covered: a `warn` guard's flag names no inputs, added when a client asks.*
- [x] **#29, `storage-schema.md` §8 says an overlap invariant compiles to an exclusion constraint on PostgreSQL**, where ADR-0213 decision 7 and the core compile none; so did §11 and `first-consumer-prd-check.md`'s R2 row. Filed 2026-10-20, verified here as far as the code goes: no `EXCLUDE` anywhere in the core; the reporter ran the issue's flow on PostgreSQL 16 and found the overlap refused `invariant_violated` with no exclusion constraint in the database, which was not repeated here. *Done 2026-10-20: the three passages corrected in place, each saying what it said. R2's row now says its "in the database itself" is not met, the engine refusing.*
  - [x] **A test of overlapping requests sent at once**, on both backends: one booking taken and the rest refused `invariant_violated`, no `5xx`. Today the core tests overlap one request after another (`an_invariant_scans_every_other_object`) and the service tests concurrent writes for lost updates (`concurrent_writes_lose_no_update`); the concurrent overlap result in `storage-schema.md` §8 was the reporter's measurement (issue #29). *Done 2026-10-21: `overlapping_bookings_sent_at_once_take_one` and `_on_postgresql`, sixteen callers released together by a barrier, one of 16 overlapping windows taken and 15 refused naming it, no `5xx`, and 16 windows apart all taken; the invariant replaced by `true`, all 16 overlapping are taken and the test fails. What keeps them apart within one service is its one writer, which holds the store; between services, a second on a file store is warned at its start and takes turns with the first on SQLite's write lock, each write's transaction begun `IMMEDIATE` (ADR-0178 decision 2, `storage-schema.md` §7), and a second on PostgreSQL is refused by the store's advisory lock. The test sends through one service. `storage-schema.md` §8 cites it.*
  - [x] **`storage-schema.md`'s PostgreSQL column checked against the backend ADR-0213 built**: its preamble (line 38) still says the column is reasoned from PostgreSQL's documentation apart from two probed behaviours, written before the backend existed. Found fixing #29, not checked further. *Done 2026-10-21, each statement checked against the code, a correction beside each one wrong, saying what it said: the backend runs the store's SQL as written for SQLite through `db.rs`'s `to_postgres`, an `INTEGER` as `BIGINT`, with one writing instance (ADR-0213 decisions 2 and 4), so §2's positions and `txn` from a sequence and `xid8` are `of_counter`'s on both, §3.1's `BOOLEAN`, `NUMERIC`, `TIMESTAMPTZ` and `DATE` are SQLite's representations as `BIGINT` and `TEXT`, §7's row locks by `FOR UPDATE` and settled read below `xmin` are not taken, writes serial and the cursor the position, as on SQLite; still true, a stored end's key naming the directory (§3). Checking it found statements false on both backends, recorded beside them and below: §8's `UNIQUE` and `CHECK` rows, which ADR-0165 decision 1 had made an index and no backend compiles; the UUIDv7 id; the sequence store's second connection and file; and an indexed derived attribute's generated column.*
- [x] **Author: an object's id.** ADR-0018 decides an id globally unique, across deployments that might be merged, so no per-type or per-table sequence, and opaque, encoding no meaning; ADR-0077 decision 7 makes it a UUIDv7 from an injected id source, deterministic in tests and examples, a UUIDv7 in production (`library-api.md:101`). The store mints `<type>-<n>` everywhere, a deployment's store included (`store.rs:5156`, from `of_counter`'s `object`; `booking-4` in `overlapping_bookings_sent_at_once_take_one`): unique in one store only, naming its type and showing how many objects were made, and on the wire, which binds from 1.0.0 (ADR-0180). Found 2026-10-21 checking `storage-schema.md` §2. Options: (a) build what was decided, a deployment's id source minting UUIDv7 and the sandbox, the examples and the tests keeping `<type>-<n>` from the injected source, an upgraded store's existing ids kept since an id never changes, so a store holds both forms, which an opaque id allows; (b) record the build as the decision, an ADR narrowing ADR-0018 to one store, two deployments never merged without renaming. **Recommended: (a), before 1.0.0**, the last release at which changing what a new id looks like breaks no promise; clients already must not parse an id. *Decided 2026-10-21 as recommended, (a) ("go with your recommendations"), and built the same day: ADR-0226, a deployment's store minting UUIDv7s from a seed read at its opening and the object's number, a sandbox's numbering as before. Running the tests with it found a restored file beside a later log refused with `--create`'s advice, now the restore's (`CHANGELOG.md`).*
- [x] **Author: the sequence store as built.** ADR-0076 puts the sequence store on a second connection, on SQLite in its own file backed up after the store; the build keeps `of_sequence` in the store on both backends, a request's draws written in its transaction or, refused, after its rollback (`store.rs`, `write_sequences`, since `d2327f9`), with no second connection or file. Behaviour is as ADR-0029 and ADR-0122 decide, numbers not reused after a refusal; one writer (ADR-0178) is what lets the store take them itself, and a restore skips numbers drawn after its copy (ADR-0207 decision 3). Found 2026-10-21 checking `storage-schema.md` §6. **Recommended: record the build**, an ADR refining ADR-0076, its second connection kept as the design for several writing instances. *Decided 2026-10-21 as recommended ("go with your recommendations"): ADR-0225, which also states the one window, a crash between a refusal's rollback and the write of its draws, where its numbers are drawn again.*
- [x] **An indexed derived attribute has no generated column**: `flow-format.md` §4.11's `indexed: true` on a derived attribute promises a query filters on it, and `storage-schema.md` §3.1 a generated column and index, which no backend makes (no `GENERATED` column in `store.rs`'s DDL). Check how a query filters on one today, whether `indexed` changes anything, and fix the document or the build. Found 2026-10-21, not checked further. *Checked 2026-10-21, the documents fixed: `indexed` on a derived attribute does what both checkers hold it to, a rule's type scan may read it (check 7) and it reads only its object's stored, indexed values and no clock (check 46); a query filters on any derived attribute, indexed or not, computing it for each object it reads a page at a time, among the analytics (`query_scans`), probed in the core with one of each; no backend makes a generated column or rewrites a clock-reading filter to its operand. `storage-schema.md` §3.1 and §3.4, `flow-format.md` §4.11's `indexed` and `declaration-syntax.md` §7 and §8 say so beside what they said; no ADR had decided the column.*
  - [ ] **An indexed derivation's index, built when a measured query needs it**: §3.4's generated column for one over the object's own stored values, and the join to `of_interval` for one comparing `entered_at` with `now`, so a query naming it reads through an index rather than its type whole. Measure first: a query filtering on a derived attribute, on the six-year store, against PRD C5's read target; a stored generated column on SQLite needs the type's table rebuilt to add it, which a publish's mapping would do. *Measured 2026-10-21 on the six-year store `build-store.py` builds (`--claims 36000`: 108,616 objects, 364,531 events, an 824 MB file; HEAD `12dbc52`, the 0.5.1 core, release build; every event recorded in the build's 24 minutes, the wall clock being a deployment's, so no claim had aged and an ageing filter matched none; one i7-8700, idle, load under 1.8), a first page of 50 read ten times, one at a time (`scripts/profile/query-cost.py`, run twice, the same within 5%): a worklist by state, an index serving it, 16 ms at the median and 20 at p95; `over_policy_lines > 0`, a derivation over a set matching 11,479 claims, 26 ms, its matches filling the page early; `over_policy_lines > 2`, matching none, 3.7 s and 3.8 s, the type read whole; `entered_at(state) + 14 days <= now`, matching none, 1.9 s; the same narrowed by `state == Claim.SUBMITTED`, 107 ms; `first_submitted_at is not null and first_submitted_at + 30 days <= now`, the object's own stored value, 388 ms. PRD C5 sets a one-object read's 100 ms and a metric's 1 s; a page of a query has no target of its own, and Gate P bounds none. Not needed at the profile, so kept deferred: the generated column would serve the 388 ms case, within a metric's second already; the join would serve the 1.9 s case, which narrowing by state brings to 107 ms, now advised in `operate.md`; and the slowest, a derivation over a set, neither serves, `operate.md`'s advice to store such a value being its answer.*
- [x] **`db.rs`'s module doc says SQLite is its one backend** "until PostgreSQL's lands behind it", which it has (ADR-0213); a comment to correct with the next change to the file. Found 2026-10-21. *Done 2026-10-21: it names both backends and `to_postgres`.*
- [x] **`SECURITY.md`'s first limit says the store is one SQLite file** served by one process, written before PostgreSQL; a store is one SQLite file or one PostgreSQL database, written by one instance (ADR-0213 decision 4). Found 2026-10-21 rewriting the README, not changed with it. *Done 2026-10-21: one writer and no standby service, on either backend, PostgreSQL's availability its own failover (ADR-0213 decision 4); the README's limits table says the same.*
- [x] **The Flow Studio's runner backend refuses a metric's `params`**, which the core binds. *Corrected 2026-10-19, before the fix: this entry said the studio's server refuses the `params` of a query, and that the core backend "would forward it"; both were read off the key list (`apps/flow-studio/server/flowstudio/http.py:45`) and not checked against the server. The server forwards a body to the core without checking its keys (`unknown and not forwarded`), so the core backend took `params` all along; the runner backend serves no query at all, its routes holding none, which the front end never asks; and what it refused was a metric's `params`, 400, since ADR-0221. No test held the key list to `wire-v1.json`.* *Fixed 2026-10-19: the reference runner binds a metric's parameters as the core does (ADR-0221), its reader's filter parsing `:name` (`flowexpr.parse_filter`), each typed by the field of the rows it meets (`Evaluator.row_reads`, the core's table), decoded as a request's input of the type, an instant in RFC 3339 alone, and refused alike; the sandbox passes `params`, 400 where it is no object; the key list takes it on a query and a metric. Tests: the runner's self-test (seventeen filters answering as the same filter written with the values, ten refusals, the personal guard; five mutants killed), `test_the_studio_takes_the_keys_the_wire_lists`, and `test_a_metrics_parameters_are_read_alike`, the same reads through both backends compared whole by the tee, which fails when the key or the sandbox's passing is dropped.*

## The guides

Decided by ADR-0193 on the author's request, 2026-10-13.

- [x] The guides rewritten for people and agents, `llms.txt`, and `scripts/check-guides.py` in `check-all`. *Done 2026-10-13: the tutorials' 27 commands compared with the service's answers; every verdict kind, remedy, reason, generated clause, read refusal, fault code, flag, metric, operation and key named, read from the source; both indexes whole; each check mutation-tested (a planted wrong answer, a removed code, a removed link, each caught); the old pages' backticked terms audited, seven omissions restored.*

## Flow Studio

The first application built on the engine (ADR-0149): an editor, viewer and simulator of flows, the test case that follows the implementation and the sample a user can read ([`docs/design/flow-studio.md`](docs/design/flow-studio.md)). No code is written before the author has reviewed the design and approved its dependencies.

- [x] **The studio presented as the engine's example application**, at the author's request (2026-10-21: "as the official example application, it should be attractive"). *Done 2026-10-21: an accent colour and cards, a header with a mark and the backend's state, full-height columns each scrolling on its own, examples listed by readable names with their identifiers as tooltips, each step's outcome a badge and the example's result green or red, a module opening on its type with the most transitions, and a lifecycle too wide for its card drawn top to bottom; the README rewritten as a tour with four screenshots, a quick start, a diagram of how the page, the server and the engine fit, and the modules it opens; the hand-test walks moved to `TESTING.md`, walks 1, 5 and 6 driven again against the core, walk 5's report brought up to date. The root README shows the studio. Typecheck, lint and format clean; screenshots taken by driving the page in Chromium.*
- [x] **The runner backend serves the front end's operations, not the whole wire**: eleven of `wire-v1.json`'s, `query`, `check`, `available`, `exceptions`, `pull`, `acknowledge`, `export`, `diagnostics`, `maintain`, `referenced` and `upgrade`, are answered `404 no operation` by it, which `flow-studio.md` said nothing of, saying both backends speak the same wire. *Found 2026-10-19 reading its routes for a metric's `params`, and confirmed by a request to each of three. Decided the same day, as within the studio's design: it stays the front end's subset, the runner the oracle for what the replay and the tee compare, and `flow-studio.md` says so; building eleven operations into the sandbox would add a second implementation of each that nothing reads.*
- [x] **A malformed body drops the connection**, found 2026-10-18 restarting the studio on 0.3.0: `/sandbox/load` given `files` as names rather than `{name, text}` raised a `TypeError` in `sandbox.py` (`checked`, `f["name"]` on a string), and the server closed the socket, which the front end's proxy answers `502`, where a `400` naming the key was due. Found by a probe of mine, not by a user; the studio's own front end sends the right shape. *Fixed 2026-10-18: a load's `files` checked for their shape, a malformed one answered `400` naming it by either backend's server, and the forwarder's own faults answered `500` as the runner's path answers them; test `test_a_load_of_another_shape_is_answered_not_dropped`, two mutants killed.*

Found 2026-10-06 by the comparison.
